## 05 cvrp_aco_r3_e265

算子 Refine；节点 254；父节点 201；fitness -11.854666471291871；局部纠错：是。

增益：把原版相互冲突的 Gaussian-like、保持 d^-3 等措辞收敛到实际 d^-2.5、角度幂函数、两节点需求线性惩罚。

保留问题或边界：二次版的主要公式和 lambda 从 min 改为平均等父代变化有代码依据；“更平滑所以搜索更好”仍是收益假设。

<details><summary>原始 Idea、校准 Idea、最终代码与父代码</summary>

### 前置 Idea

The current algorithm relies heavily on a "sweep" heuristic (angular continuity) and hard capacity masks, which can sometimes trap ants in sub-optimal local minima or suppress valid inter-cluster connections. To improve, I propose a hybrid heuristic that:
1.  **Softens the Angular Bias**: Instead of a hard exponential penalty based on a fixed cosine clamp, I use a Gaussian-like smoothness factor that penalizes angular deviation more gracefully, avoiding the cliff effect where edges are effectively zeroed out.
2.  **Adds a Demand-Awareness Term**: The previous version used a hard mask for infeasible pairs (sum of demands > capacity). While necessary for feasibility in the ant's choice, the heuristic can guide the ant *away* from edges that *will* likely lead to infeasibility in subsequent steps by penalizing edges that consume a high fraction of the remaining capacity relative to the average remaining demand of unvisited nodes. This acts as a soft load-balancing signal.
3.  **Refines Distance Scaling**: I maintain the inverse cubic distance but add a small epsilon to the diagonal to ensure numerical stability and avoid division by zero issues in edge cases, while normalizing more robustly.
4.  **Dynamic Capacity Heuristic**: I compute a "capacity feasibility score" for each edge based on the demand of the two nodes versus the capacity. Edges that leave very little remaining capacity are slightly down-weighted compared to those that leave more slack, encouraging balanced routes.

Key changes from the current best:
- Replace `exp(lambda * cos)` with a smoother `(1 + cos) / 2` based penalty scaled by density, to avoid over-penalization.
- Add a soft penalty for edges that consume a large fraction of the capacity, encouraging ants to save capacity for later (better global packing).
- Slightly adjust the distance exponent to 2.5 instead of 3 to allow a bit more exploration of medium-range edges if local ones are blocked by capacity.

### 校准 Idea

The implementation constructs an edge-prior matrix by multiplying three distinct heuristic components: a distance-based preference, an angular continuity score, and a capacity-awareness factor. The distance component uses an inverse power law with an exponent of 2.5, specifically $1 / (d_{ij}^{2.5})$, normalized by the maximum value. This moderate exponent balances the strong preference for short edges found in the parent’s exponent 3.0 with increased tolerance for medium-range connections, preventing the heuristic from overly suppressing edges that might be necessary for global routing. The angular component replaces the parent’s exponential cosine penalty with a smooth power-law function based on the angular score $0.5(1 + \cos \theta_{ij})$. For customer-to-customer edges, this score is raised to a dynamic lambda power, where lambda is derived from local angular density. Nodes with high density (small minimum angular separation) receive higher lambda values (up to 3.0), enforcing strict sweep continuity, while sparse areas receive lower lambda (down to 0.5), allowing more flexibility. Depot edges are assigned a neutral angular factor of 1.0 to avoid biasing the start or end of routes. The capacity component introduces a soft load-balancing penalty calculated as $1 - 0.5 \cdot (d_i + d_j) / C$. This linearly reduces the desirability of edges that consume a larger fraction of the vehicle capacity, encouraging ants to select pairs that leave sufficient slack for subsequent customers. Edges exceeding the capacity are hard-masked to zero. The final matrix is normalized by the minimum positive value and floored at $10^{-9}$ to ensure numerical stability and valid sampling probabilities.

Key implementation changes relative to the parent code include the modification of the distance exponent from 3.0 to 2.5, which softens the distance decay to allow slightly longer edges to remain competitive. The angular heuristic is significantly altered: the parent used an exponential function $e^{\lambda \cos \theta}$ with a complex adaptive lambda range (1.0 to 8.0) and distance-based relaxation, whereas the final code uses a power function $(0.5(1+\cos \theta))^\lambda$ with a fixed, narrower lambda range (0.5 to 3.0) based solely on angular density. This change removes the distance-dependent relaxation of the sweep bias, simplifying the logic and potentially preventing over-relaxation for long edges. The capacity heuristic is refined from a mild linear discount (factor $1 - 0.25 \cdot \text{ratio}$) to a stronger linear penalty (factor $1 - 0.5 \cdot \text{ratio}$), more aggressively discouraging high-load pairs to improve route packing efficiency. Finally, the lambda aggregation for customer-customer edges changes from taking the minimum of the two nodes' local lambdas to the average, providing a balanced constraint rather than a relaxed one. These changes aim to reduce the risk of trapping ants in local minima by smoothing the heuristic landscape and better balancing the trade-offs between distance, continuity, and capacity utilization.

### 最终代码

```python
import numpy as np

def heuristics(distance_matrix: np.ndarray, coordinates: np.ndarray, demands: np.ndarray, capacity: int) -> np.ndarray:
    """Return edge desirability values for CVRP ant colony optimization.

    Args:
        distance_matrix: Pairwise Euclidean distances with shape (n, n).
        coordinates: Node coordinates with shape (n, 2). Node 0 is the depot.
        demands: Node demands with shape (n,). The depot demand is zero.
        capacity: Capacity shared by all vehicles.

    Returns:
        An (n, n) edge-prior matrix. Larger values make an edge more likely
        to be sampled. Values at or below zero are treated as 1e-9.
    """
    n = distance_matrix.shape[0]
    if n <= 1:
        return np.full((n, n), 1.0, dtype=float)

    eps = 1e-12
    floor_val = 1e-9

    # 1. Distance Component: Inverse power law
    # Using exponent 2.5 to balance short-path preference with some tolerance for longer links
    dist_safe = np.where(distance_matrix > eps, distance_matrix, 1.0)
    dist_base = 1.0 / (dist_safe ** 2.5)
    max_dist_base = np.max(dist_base)
    if max_dist_base > eps:
        dist_base = dist_base / max_dist_base

    # 2. Angular Component: Smooth sweep heuristic
    depot = coordinates[0]
    vecs = coordinates - depot
    mags = np.linalg.norm(vecs, axis=1)

    safe_vecs = np.where(mags[:, np.newaxis] > eps, vecs, 0.0)
    safe_mags = np.where(mags > eps, mags, 1.0)
    unit_vecs = safe_vecs / safe_mags[:, np.newaxis]

    cos_mat = unit_vecs @ unit_vecs.T
    # Clip cosine to avoid extreme values, but keep it in a reasonable range
    cos_mat = np.clip(cos_mat, -1.0, 1.0)

    # Compute local angular density for each customer (nodes 1 to n-1)
    num_customers = n - 1
    lambda_matrix = np.ones((n, n), dtype=float)

    if num_customers > 1:
        angles = np.arctan2(unit_vecs[1:, 1], unit_vecs[1:, 0])

        # Calculate minimum angular difference to any other customer
        a_i = angles[:, np.newaxis]
        a_j = angles[np.newaxis, :]
        diff = np.abs(a_i - a_j)
        diff = np.minimum(diff, 2 * np.pi - diff)
        np.fill_diagonal(diff, np.inf)

        min_angular_diff = np.min(diff, axis=1)

        # Map density to lambda
        # High density (small diff) -> High lambda (strict sweep)
        # Low density (large diff) -> Low lambda (relaxed)
        thresh_sparse = np.pi / 3
        thresh_dense = np.pi / 12

        denom = thresh_sparse - thresh_dense
        if denom > eps:
            t = (thresh_sparse - min_angular_diff) / denom
        else:
            t = np.zeros_like(min_angular_diff)

        t = np.clip(t, 0.0, 1.0)

        # Lambda ranges from 0.5 (relaxed) to 3.0 (strict)
        # Using a softer range than before to avoid over-constraining
        local_lambdas = 0.5 + t * (3.0 - 0.5)

        # For customer-customer edges, use the average lambda of the two nodes
        # This is a compromise between min (too relaxed) and max (too strict)
        lc = local_lambdas[:, np.newaxis]
        lc_t = local_lambdas[np.newaxis, :]
        lambda_matrix[1:, 1:] = (lc + lc_t) / 2.0

    # Apply lambda to cosine
    # The angular factor is exp(lambda * (cos - 1) / 2) ? No, let's stick to a simpler form.
    # Let's use: angular_factor = exp(lambda * cos) but with a smaller lambda range.
    # To be smoother, we can map cos from [-1, 1] to [0, 1] and then apply power.
    # angular_score = 0.5 * (1 + cos)  # Range [0, 1], 1 if same direction, 0 if opposite
    # factor = angular_score ** lambda

    angular_score = 0.5 * (1.0 + cos_mat)
    # Ensure angular_score is positive for power
    angular_score = np.maximum(angular_score, 1e-6)

    # Depot edges should have no angular bias
    angular_factor = np.ones((n, n), dtype=float)
    if num_customers > 0:
        customer_mask = np.ones((n, n), dtype=bool)
        customer_mask[0, :] = False
        customer_mask[:, 0] = False
        customer_mask[0, 0] = False

        # Use lambda_matrix for the power
        # Higher lambda means the factor drops off faster for non-aligned edges
        angular_factor[customer_mask] = angular_score[customer_mask] ** lambda_matrix[customer_mask]

        # Normalize angular factor to [0, 1] or similar?
        # The max is 1 (cos=1). Min is 0 (cos=-1).
        # This is good.

    # 3. Capacity Heuristic: Soft load balancing
    # Penalize edges that consume too much capacity
    cap_float = float(capacity)
    d = demands.astype(float)
    pair_demand = d[:, np.newaxis] + d[np.newaxis, :]

    # Hard infeasibility mask
    infeasible = pair_demand > cap_float
    np.fill_diagonal(infeasible, True)

    # Soft penalty: If pair_demand is close to capacity, reduce heuristic
    # ratio = pair_demand / capacity
    # penalty = 1 - (ratio ** 2) ?
    # Let's use a smooth step: if ratio > 0.8, start penalizing
    # Or simply: factor = 1 - 0.5 * (pair_demand / capacity) for feasible edges
    # This encourages leaving some capacity for later

    capacity_factor = np.ones((n, n), dtype=float)
    if capacity > 0:
        ratio = np.minimum(pair_demand / cap_float, 1.0)
        # Apply a mild penalty for high load
        # If ratio is 0, factor is 1. If ratio is 1, factor is 0.5
        capacity_factor = 1.0 - 0.5 * ratio

        # Zero out infeasible edges in capacity factor
        capacity_factor[infeasible] = 0.0

    # 4. Combine Components
    # H = dist_base * angular_factor * capacity_factor
    H = dist_base * angular_factor * capacity_factor

    # Ensure H is finite
    H = np.where(np.isfinite(H), H, 0.0)

    # Mask infeasible edges to 0 (just in case, though capacity_factor handles it)
    H[infeasible] = 0.0

    # Normalize
    positive_H = H[H > eps]
    if len(positive_H) > 0:
        min_h = np.min(positive_H)
        if min_h > eps:
            H = H / min_h
    else:
        # If no positive entries, return uniform (should not happen for valid instances)
        H = np.ones((n, n), dtype=float)
        H[infeasible] = 1e-9
        np.fill_diagonal(H, 1e-9)
        return H

    # Floor
    H = np.maximum(H, floor_val)

    return H
```

### 父代码

```python
import numpy as np

def heuristics(distance_matrix: np.ndarray, coordinates: np.ndarray, demands: np.ndarray, capacity: int) -> np.ndarray:
    """Return edge desirability values for CVRP ant colony optimization.

    Args:
        distance_matrix: Pairwise Euclidean distances with shape (n, n).
        coordinates: Node coordinates with shape (n, 2). Node 0 is the depot.
        demands: Node demands with shape (n,). The depot demand is zero.
        capacity: Capacity shared by all vehicles.

    Returns:
        An (n, n) edge-prior matrix. Larger values make an edge more likely
        to be sampled. Values at or below zero are treated as 1e-9.
    """
    n = distance_matrix.shape[0]
    if n <= 1:
        return np.full((n, n), 1.0, dtype=float)

    eps = 1e-12
    floor_val = 1e-9

    # 1. Distance Component: Inverse cube to strongly prefer short edges
    dist_safe = np.where(distance_matrix > eps, distance_matrix, 1.0)
    dist_base = 1.0 / (dist_safe ** 3)
    max_dist_base = np.max(dist_base)
    if max_dist_base > eps:
        dist_base = dist_base / max_dist_base

    # 2. Angular Component Setup
    depot = coordinates[0]
    vecs = coordinates - depot
    mags = np.linalg.norm(vecs, axis=1)

    safe_vecs = np.where(mags[:, np.newaxis] > eps, vecs, 0.0)
    safe_mags = np.where(mags > eps, mags, 1.0)
    unit_vecs = safe_vecs / safe_mags[:, np.newaxis]

    # Cosine of angle between vectors from depot
    cos_mat = unit_vecs @ unit_vecs.T
    # Clamp to [-0.5, 1.0] to allow moderate backtracking but penalize strong reversal
    cos_mat = np.clip(cos_mat, -0.5, 1.0)

    # 3. Adaptive Lambda based on Angular Density
    num_customers = n - 1
    lambda_max = 8.0
    lambda_min = 1.0

    lambda_matrix = np.full((n, n), lambda_min, dtype=float)

    if num_customers > 1:
        # Calculate angles for customers (excluding depot)
        angles = np.arctan2(unit_vecs[1:, 1], unit_vecs[1:, 0])

        a_i = angles[:, np.newaxis]
        a_j = angles[np.newaxis, :]
        diff = np.abs(a_i - a_j)
        diff = np.minimum(diff, 2 * np.pi - diff)
        np.fill_diagonal(diff, np.inf)

        # Minimum angular difference to any other customer
        min_angular_diff = np.min(diff, axis=1)

        # Map angular difference to lambda
        # Dense (small diff) -> High Lambda
        # Sparse (large diff) -> Low Lambda
        thresh_sparse = np.pi / 4
        thresh_dense = np.pi / 16
        denom = thresh_sparse - thresh_dense
        if denom > eps:
            t = (thresh_sparse - min_angular_diff) / denom
        else:
            t = np.zeros_like(min_angular_diff)

        t = np.clip(t, 0.0, 1.0)
        local_lambdas = lambda_min + t * (lambda_max - lambda_min)

        # For an edge i-j, use the minimum of the two local lambdas.
        # This ensures that if one node is sparse, the constraint is relaxed.
        # Using min avoids over-constraining edges that connect dense to sparse areas.
        lc = local_lambdas[:, np.newaxis]
        lc_t = local_lambdas[np.newaxis, :]
        lambda_matrix[1:, 1:] = np.minimum(lc, lc_t)

    # 4. Distance-Aware Relaxation of Lambda
    # Relax angular constraints for longer edges to allow inter-cluster jumps
    min_dist = np.min(dist_safe)
    max_dist = np.max(dist_safe)
    if max_dist > min_dist:
        d_norm = (dist_safe - min_dist) / (max_dist - min_dist)
    else:
        d_norm = np.zeros_like(dist_safe)

    alpha_relax = 0.4
    lambda_effective = lambda_matrix * (1.0 - alpha_relax * d_norm)
    # Ensure lambda doesn't drop too low, maintaining some sweep preference
    lambda_effective = np.maximum(lambda_effective, 0.8)

    # Depot edges should not have strong angular bias
    lambda_effective[0, :] = lambda_min
    lambda_effective[:, 0] = lambda_min

    # 5. Combine Distance and Angular
    angular = np.exp(lambda_effective * cos_mat)

    # Neutralize depot edges and diagonal
    angular[0, :] = 1.0
    angular[:, 0] = 1.0
    np.fill_diagonal(angular, 1.0)

    H = dist_base * angular

    # 6. Capacity Handling
    if capacity > 0:
        cap_float = float(capacity)
        d = demands.astype(float)
        pair_demand = d[:, np.newaxis] + d[np.newaxis, :]

        # Hard mask: Infeasible pairs
        infeasible = pair_demand > cap_float
        # Also check if single node exceeds capacity (shouldn't happen for valid problems, but safe)
        single_infeasible = (d > cap_float)[:, np.newaxis] | (d > cap_float)[np.newaxis, :]
        infeasible = infeasible | single_infeasible
        np.fill_diagonal(infeasible, True)

        H[infeasible] = 0.0

        # Soft penalty: Mild linear discount for feasible customer-customer edges
        # Discourages edges that fill the vehicle completely
        customer_mask = (np.arange(n)[:, np.newaxis] > 0) & (np.arange(n)[np.newaxis, :] > 0)
        feasible_customers = (~infeasible) & customer_mask

        if np.any(feasible_customers):
            ratio = np.clip(pair_demand / cap_float, 0.0, 1.0)
            # soft factor: 1.0 at ratio 0, 0.75 at ratio 1
            soft = 1.0 - 0.25 * ratio
            H[feasible_customers] *= soft[feasible_customers]

    # 7. Final Sanitization and Normalization
    np.fill_diagonal(H, 0.0)
    H = np.where(np.isfinite(H), H, 0.0)

    positive_H = H[H > eps]
    if len(positive_H) > 0:
        min_h = np.min(positive_H)
        if min_h > eps:
            H = H / min_h

    H = np.maximum(H, floor_val)
    return H
```

</details>

## 06 online_bin_packing_r1_e121

算子 Refine；节点 120；父节点 35；fitness -744.5；局部纠错：是。

增益：原版公式写 -lambda*item/bins、lambda=0.001 且方向解释混乱；二次版写对 bins/item 和 1e-6，并改称 Best Fit。

保留问题或边界：两版均保留不存在的破平局收益；在 item>0 的有限常规输入上，s=-(1+eps/item)*bins+item，排序仍是 Best Fit。二次版还误称更大 item 会产生更大的该项惩罚。

<details><summary>原始 Idea、校准 Idea、最终代码与父代码</summary>

### 前置 Idea

The algorithm implements a refined "Best Fit Decreasing" variant that prioritizes minimizing waste (gap) while incorporating a secondary heuristic to favor bins that, after placement, leave the smallest residual capacity. This is achieved by calculating the "new gap" ($bin_{capacity} - item$) and using its negative as the primary score. To improve upon standard Best Fit, I add a secondary term that penalizes bins with *larger* total capacities relative to the item, effectively preferring bins that are "just big enough". Specifically, the score is $-(bin_{capacity} - item) - \lambda \cdot \frac{item}{bin_{capacity}}$. The second term ($-\lambda \cdot \frac{item}{bin_{capacity}}$) is negative, so bins with *smaller* capacities (higher ratio) get a less negative (higher) score, reinforcing the tight-fit preference. However, a more robust improvement for online bin packing is to look ahead: prefer bins that will leave a remainder that is difficult to use (i.e., small) or that matches the size of upcoming items if known (not applicable here). A better standard improvement is to use a weighted sum of the negative gap and a penalty on the *relative* size of the bin, ensuring that among nearly equal fits, the bin with the smallest absolute capacity is chosen to preserve larger bins for future large items. I will use a small constant $\lambda = 0.001$ for the secondary term to ensure it only breaks ties without overriding the primary gap minimization, but I will also add a small bonus for bins that result in a *smaller* residual capacity, which is equivalent to the primary term. The key change is to ensure strict monotonicity for the primary term and use a deterministic, meaningful tie-breaker.

### 校准 Idea

The implemented algorithm utilizes a composite scoring function to determine the optimal bin for an incoming item in an online one-dimensional bin packing scenario. The primary objective is to minimize the total number of bins used, which aligns with the "Best Fit" heuristic. The function accepts the size of the current item and an array of remaining capacities for all feasible bins (where the remaining capacity is at least the item size). The core calculation involves converting the integer array of bin capacities to a floating-point format to ensure numerical stability and prevent integer overflow or division errors. The primary component of the priority score is the negative of the remaining gap, defined as `-(bin_capacity - item_size)`. By maximizing this value, the algorithm effectively minimizes the unused space left in the bin after placement. This directly implements the Best Fit strategy, which is known to perform well by tightly packing items and reducing fragmentation.

To handle potential ties or subtle differences in fit quality, a secondary penalty term is introduced. This term is calculated as `epsilon * (bin_capacity / item_size)`, where `epsilon` is a small constant set to `1e-6`. This relative penalty slightly discourages placing items into bins with larger capacities relative to the item size, provided the primary gap differences are negligible. The division by item size normalizes the penalty, ensuring that larger items face a proportionally larger penalty for using oversized bins. To ensure robustness, the code uses `np.errstate` to suppress warnings during division and `np.nan_to_num` to replace any resulting NaN or infinite values with zero, safeguarding against edge cases where the item size or bin capacity might be zero, although the problem statement implies feasible bins have non-zero capacity.

The final score returned for each bin is the sum of the primary negative gap and the negative of the secondary penalty. The item is assigned to the bin with the highest priority score. This approach prioritizes the smallest feasible bin, which is a standard and effective heuristic for online bin packing. The secondary term acts as a tie-breaker that favors smaller bins even when the absolute gaps are very close, potentially preserving larger bins for future items that might be too large for the smaller ones. The use of a very small epsilon ensures that the primary Best Fit logic dominates the decision process, while the secondary term provides a deterministic and physically meaningful preference for tighter fits relative to the item size. This balances the immediate goal of minimizing waste with a long-term strategy of maintaining flexible bin capacities for future arrivals.

### 最终代码

```python
import numpy as np

def priority(item: float, bins: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    """Returns priority with which we want to add item to each bin.
    Args:
        item: Size of item to be added to the bin.
        bins: Array of capacities for each bin.
    Return:
        Array of same size as bins with priority score of each bin.
    """
    bins_float = bins.astype(float)

    # Primary metric: Best Fit - minimize the remaining space (gap)
    # We want to maximize the priority, so we minimize the gap.
    # score = -gap = -(bins - item)
    gaps = bins_float - item
    base_score = -gaps

    # Secondary metric: Tie-breaker
    # If gaps are identical (e.g., bins of different sizes but same gap? No, if item is fixed,
    # bins must be identical for gaps to be identical).
    # Actually, if bins are integers, gaps are integers. Ties happen when multiple bins have
    # the exact same remaining capacity. In that case, it doesn't matter which we pick.
    # However, if we consider floating point noise or if the problem allows for bins that
    # were previously packed differently, maybe we prefer the bin with the smaller
    # original capacity? No, we only see remaining capacity.

    # A common improvement is to prefer bins that will result in a "cleaner" state or
    # to avoid creating tiny unusable gaps. But standard Best Fit is usually strong.

    # Let's try a weighted approach:
    # Score = - (bins - item) - epsilon * (bins / item)
    # This slightly penalizes larger bins even if the gap is the same (which can't happen for fixed item).
    # Wait, if gaps are the same, bins are the same.

    # Let's look at the distribution. If we have bins [10, 11] and item 5.
    # Gaps: 5, 6. Best fit is 10. Score: -5 vs -6. 10 wins.

    # What if we want to save larger bins? Best Fit does this.

    # Maybe the issue is that pure Best Fit can be suboptimal if it creates a tiny gap that is
    # useless. But without future knowledge, Best Fit is standard.

    # Let's add a very small penalty for the bin size itself to break any potential floating point
    # ties or to prefer smaller bins in case of exact integer ties (which are identical bins).
    # Actually, if two bins have the same remaining capacity, they are interchangeable.

    # Let's try to incorporate the "relative fill" aspect.
    # Some heuristics use: score = item / bins (Worst Fit is max, Best Fit is min).
    # Current code uses -gap which is equivalent to preferring smallest bins.

    # Let's refine the tie-breaker to be more robust. If gaps are very close,
    # prefer the bin with the smaller absolute capacity (to save larger capacity bins).
    # Since -gap already does this primarily, the secondary term should only kick in
    # when gaps are effectively equal.

    # To make the secondary term meaningful only when gaps are close, we can't easily
    # do conditional logic in vectorized numpy efficiently without masking.

    # Let's just use a small penalty on the bin capacity itself.
    # score = - (bins - item) - 1e-6 * bins
    # This ensures that if gaps are identical, the smaller bin is preferred.
    # If gaps are different by 1, the primary term difference is 1. The secondary term
    # difference is 1e-6 * diff_bins. If diff_bins is 1000, diff is 1e-3.
    # So primary dominates. Good.

    eps = 1e-6

    with np.errstate(divide='ignore', invalid='ignore'):
        # Avoid division by zero if item is 0 or bins are 0 (bins should be >= item > 0)
        rel_penalty = bins_float / item

    rel_penalty = np.nan_to_num(rel_penalty, nan=0.0, posinf=0.0, neginf=0.0)

    return base_score - eps * rel_penalty
```

### 父代码

```python
import numpy as np

def priority(item: float, bins: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    """Returns priority with which we want to add item to each bin.
    Args:
        item: Size of item to be added to the bin.
        bins: Array of capacities for each bin.
    Return:
        Array of same size as bins with priority score of each bin.
    """
    bins_float = bins.astype(float)

    # Base priority: Best Fit (minimize remaining space after placement)
    # A smaller gap means a higher (less negative) priority.
    gaps = bins_float - item
    base_priority = -gaps

    # Secondary tie-breaker:
    # Use the ratio item/bins as a proxy for tightness.
    # For a fixed item, smaller bins have higher item/bins ratio.
    # We add a tiny amount of this to favor tighter bins in case of ties.
    # We scale by epsilon to ensure it's strictly secondary to the base priority.
    # Note: item/bins is positive, so adding it increases priority for smaller bins.
    eps = np.finfo(float).eps
    # Avoid division by zero if bins contains 0, though problem states "feasible bins"
    # so bins >= item > 0. But safe guard just in case.
    with np.errstate(divide='ignore', invalid='ignore'):
        ratio_term = item / bins_float

    # If any bins were 0, ratio_term would be inf or nan.
    # However, "feasible bins" implies bins >= item. If item > 0, bins > 0.
    # If item == 0, ratio is 0.
    # We replace non-finite values with 0 to keep it safe, though likely unnecessary.
    ratio_term = np.nan_to_num(ratio_term, nan=0.0, posinf=0.0, neginf=0.0)

    tie_breaker = eps * ratio_term

    return base_priority + tie_breaker
```

</details>

## 07 online_bin_packing_r1_e266

算子 Refine；节点 265；父节点 198；fitness -740.25；局部纠错：是。

增益：二次版写对大间隙阈值由 2.5*item 改为 2*item，以及实际惩罚、bonus 权重。

保留问题或边界：两版均说 Gaussian sigma=0.5*item；代码是 max(item,1)，item=10 时实际 sigma=10，描述为 5。

<details><summary>原始 Idea、校准 Idea、最终代码与父代码</summary>

### 前置 Idea

The current algorithm uses a bounded best-fit score $1/(1+\text{gap})$ which heavily favors tight fits, but this can lead to the creation of small, unusable "dead zone" gaps if the fit is not perfect. To improve this, I will:
1.  **Strengthen the Dead Zone Penalty**: Increase the weight of the penalty for gaps in the range $(0, 0.5 \times \text{item})$. The previous penalty was quadratic but relatively weak. A stronger, perhaps linear or sharper quadratic penalty, will force the algorithm to avoid creating slivers. If a bin cannot accommodate the item with zero waste or a waste large enough to be useful (i.e., $\ge 0.5 \times \text{item}$), it should be strongly discouraged.
2.  **Refine the Compatibility Bonus**: The Gaussian bonus is centered at $1.2 \times \text{item}$. This encourages leaving space for another item. I will keep this but ensure it doesn't override the dead-zone avoidance. I might shift the center slightly to $1.0 \times \text{item}$ to be more direct about "one more item" potential, or keep $1.2$ if "slightly larger" items are more common. Given integer inputs, $1.0 \times \text{item}$ is a strong target for "exactly one more item". Let's try centering at $1.0 \times \text{item}$ with a standard deviation of $0.5 \times \text{item}$ to make it sharper.
3.  **Base Score Adjustment**: The base score $1/(1+\text{gap})$ saturates quickly. For integer gaps, $1/(1+0)=1$, $1/(1+1)=0.5$. The drop is significant. However, for larger items, the relative difference between gaps of 1 and 2 is small in absolute terms but large in relative terms. The current formulation is robust. I will keep it but ensure the secondary terms are balanced against it.
4.  **Large Gap Penalty**: Keep the gentle quadratic penalty for gaps $> 2.5 \times \text{item}$ to save large bins.

Key change: A more aggressive "no-sliver" policy. If the gap is small but non-zero, it is essentially wasted space. The algorithm should prefer a slightly larger gap that might be useful over a tiny wasted gap, UNLESS the tiny gap is 0 (perfect fit). Wait, actually, in bin packing, a gap of 0 is great. A gap of 1 (if item size is 10) is bad. A gap of 10 is good. A gap of 1 is bad. The "dead zone" is where the gap is too small to hold another typical item but too large to be "negligible" (though with integers, 1 is small). The heuristic should prefer gap=0 or gap >= item_size.

Let's modify the dead zone penalty to be very strong for $0 < \text{gap} < \text{item}$. Actually, let's just penalize any gap that is not 0 or large enough to hold another item.

### 校准 Idea

The implementation utilizes a composite scoring function to select the optimal bin for an incoming item in a one-dimensional online bin packing problem. The core decision rule assigns a priority score to each feasible bin (where remaining capacity $\ge$ item size) and selects the bin with the highest score. The score is derived from four distinct components that balance tight packing, future compatibility, and fragmentation avoidance. First, a base score calculated as $1/(1 + \text{gap})$, where $\text{gap}$ is the remaining capacity after placing the item, ensures that bins with the least waste are generally preferred, consistent with Best-Fit heuristics. Second, a Gaussian compatibility bonus rewards gaps centered exactly at one item size ($1.0 \times \text{item}$) with a standard deviation of $0.5 \times \text{item}$, encouraging the retention of space large enough for another item of similar magnitude. Third, a "dead zone" penalty aggressively penalizes gaps that are non-zero but smaller than the item size ($0 < \text{gap} < \text{item}$). This quadratic penalty, weighted at 1.5, is designed to discourage the creation of unusable slivers that cannot accommodate future items, effectively forcing the algorithm to prefer either a perfect fit (gap=0) or a gap large enough to be useful. Finally, a gentle quadratic penalty is applied to gaps exceeding $2.0 \times \text{item}$, preserving large bins for future large items. This approach hypothesizes that by explicitly distinguishing between "useful" empty space (approx. item size) and "dead" space (small fragments), the algorithm will minimize bin usage more effectively than pure best-fit strategies that inadvertently create fragmented waste.

The important implementation changes relative to the parent code involve a strategic shift in the handling of empty space and penalty weighting to improve fragmentation resistance. The most significant change is the expansion of the "dead zone" penalty range from $(0, 0.5 \times \text{item})$ to $(0, 1.0 \times \text{item})$ in the parent code's original logic, coupled with a substantial increase in its weight from 0.8 to 1.5. This modification ensures that any gap that cannot hold another item of the current size is strongly discouraged, regardless of how small it is. In the parent code, the compatibility bonus was centered at $1.2 \times \text{item}$ with a weaker weight of 0.6, which could potentially conflict with the best-fit tendency. The final code shifts this center to $1.0 \times \text{item}$ and increases the weight to 0.8, aligning the bonus directly with the goal of leaving space for one more item of the same size. Additionally, the large gap penalty threshold was lowered from $2.5 \times \text{item}$ to $2.0 \times \text{item}$ with a slightly higher weight (0.01 vs 0.005), making the algorithm more conservative in assigning small items to very large bins. These adjustments collectively refine the heuristic to be more explicit about "useful" versus "waste" space, aiming to reduce the number of bins by preventing the accumulation of unusable fragments.

### 最终代码

```python
import numpy as np

def priority(item: float, bins: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    """Returns priority with which we want to add item to each bin.
    Args:
        item: Size of item to be added to the bin.
        bins: Array of capacities for each bin.
    Return:
        Array of same size as bins with priority score of each bin.
    """
    item_f = float(item)
    if item_f < 1e-9:
        item_f = 1e-9

    bins_f = np.asarray(bins, dtype=np.float64)

    gap = np.maximum(bins_f - item_f, 0.0)

    # Base score: Bounded Best-Fit. Higher is better (smaller gap).
    # 1/(1+gap) gives 1 for gap=0, 0.5 for gap=1, etc.
    base_score = 1.0 / (1.0 + gap)

    # Compatibility Bonus: Gaussian centered at gap = item.
    # This rewards bins that can hold another item of similar size.
    # Center at 1.0 * item, sigma = 0.5 * item.
    sigma = max(item_f, 1.0)
    dist = gap - 1.0 * item_f
    compat_bonus = 0.8 * np.exp(-0.5 * (dist / sigma) ** 2)

    # Dead Zone Penalty: Penalize gaps that are non-zero but too small to be useful.
    # "Useful" is defined as >= item_f (can hold another item).
    # So penalize 0 < gap < item_f.
    # We want to avoid creating fragments that are too small to use.
    # A strong penalty here will push the selection towards gap=0 or gap>=item_f.
    dead_zone_max = item_f
    if dead_zone_max > 1e-6:
        in_dead_zone = (gap > 1e-6) & (gap < dead_zone_max)
        # Normalize gap in the dead zone [0, item_f] to [0, 1]
        norm_gap = np.where(in_dead_zone, gap / dead_zone_max, 0.0)
        # Quadratic penalty: 0 at 0, max at item_f.
        # Weight 1.5 is strong enough to override the base_score difference between gap=0 and gap=item_f/2.
        # Base score at gap=0 is 1.0. Base score at gap=item_f/2 is 1/(1+item_f/2).
        # For item_f=1, gap=0.5, base=1/1.5=0.66. Diff=0.33.
        # Penalty at gap=0.5 (norm=0.5) = 1.5 * 0.25 = 0.375.
        # So total score at gap=0.5 is 0.66 - 0.375 + bonus.
        # Total score at gap=0 is 1.0 + bonus(0). Bonus(0) for center=1 is exp(-0.5*(1/1)^2)=exp(-0.5)=0.6.
        # So score(0) = 1.0 + 0.8*0.6 = 1.48.
        # score(0.5) = 0.66 - 0.375 + 0.8*exp(-0.5*(0.5/1)^2) = 0.285 + 0.8*0.88 = 0.285 + 0.70 = 0.985.
        # Gap=0 is still preferred. But what about gap=1.0 (item_f=1)?
        # Base=0.5. Bonus=0.8. Total = 1.3.
        # Gap=0: 1.48. Gap=1: 1.3. Gap=0.5: 0.985.
        # This correctly prefers 0 over 0.5 and 1 over 0.5.
        # It prefers 0 over 1 slightly. This is good for packing.
        dead_penalty = 1.5 * np.where(in_dead_zone, norm_gap ** 2, 0.0)
    else:
        dead_penalty = np.zeros_like(gap)

    # Large Gap Penalty: Penalize gaps that are much larger than item_f.
    # Threshold 2.0 * item_f.
    threshold = 2.0 * item_f
    excess = np.maximum(gap - threshold, 0.0)
    scale = max(item_f, 1.0)
    large_penalty = 0.01 * (excess / scale) ** 2

    total_score = base_score + compat_bonus - dead_penalty - large_penalty

    total_score = np.nan_to_num(total_score, nan=0.0, posinf=1e10, neginf=-1e10)

    return total_score
```

### 父代码

```python
import numpy as np

def priority(item: float, bins: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    """Returns priority with which we want to add item to each bin.
    Args:
        item: Size of item to be added to the bin.
        bins: Array of capacities for each bin.
    Return:
        Array of same size as bins with priority score of each bin.
    """
    item_f = float(item)
    if item_f < 1e-9:
        item_f = 1e-9

    bins_f = np.asarray(bins, dtype=np.float64)

    # Calculate gap: remaining space after placing item, clipped to non-negative
    # Feasible bins have gap >= 0
    gap = np.maximum(bins_f - item_f, 0.0)

    # 1. Base Score: Bounded Best-Fit
    # Monotonically decreasing with gap. Bounded between 0 and 1.
    # Prevents the 1/gap explosion for very small gaps.
    base_score = 1.0 / (1.0 + gap)

    # 2. Compatibility Bonus: Gaussian centered at 1.2 * item
    # Rewards gaps that can likely hold another item of similar size.
    # Sigma scaled by item size to maintain relative width.
    sigma = max(item_f, 1.0)
    dist = gap - 1.2 * item_f
    compat_bonus = 0.6 * np.exp(-0.5 * (dist / sigma) ** 2)

    # 3. Dead Zone Penalty: Anti-fragmentation
    # Penalizes gaps that are too small to be useful (> 0) but too large to ignore (< 0.5 * item).
    # This corrects the strict Best-Fit tendency to create unusable slivers.
    dead_zone_max = 0.5 * item_f
    if dead_zone_max > 1e-6:
        in_dead_zone = (gap > 1e-6) & (gap < dead_zone_max)
        norm_gap = np.where(in_dead_zone, gap / dead_zone_max, 0.0)
        dead_penalty = 0.8 * np.where(in_dead_zone, norm_gap ** 2, 0.0)
    else:
        dead_penalty = np.zeros_like(gap)

    # 4. Large Gap Penalty: Preserve large bins
    # Gentle quadratic penalty for gaps significantly larger than 2.5 * item.
    # Prevents placing small items in very large bins, saving them for future large items.
    threshold = 2.5 * item_f
    excess = np.maximum(gap - threshold, 0.0)
    scale = max(item_f, 1.0)
    large_penalty = 0.005 * (excess / scale) ** 2

    # Combine components
    total_score = base_score + compat_bonus - dead_penalty - large_penalty

    # Sanitize output to ensure finite floating-point values
    total_score = np.nan_to_num(total_score, nan=0.0, posinf=1e10, neginf=-1e10)

    return total_score
```

</details>

## 08 online_bin_packing_r2_e138

算子 Fuse；节点 137；父节点 130；fitness -744.5；局部纠错：未计入。

增益：整理出确定公式 -r^2-0.5r 和小 item、空数组、非有限值分支。

保留问题或边界：两版均把保持严格 Best Fit 排序的变换说成克服 Best Fit 的泛化缺陷；二次版还说降低二次项影响，父代系数是 0.1，新系数为 1，不能直接作此解释。

<details><summary>原始 Idea、校准 Idea、最终代码与父代码</summary>

### 前置 Idea

The core of bin packing efficiency is minimizing the "wasted space" (fragmentation) while balancing absolute size vs. relative size. The current algorithm relies on `primary_score = -remainder` (absolute Best Fit), which fails to scale when item sizes vary drastically. The reference algorithm uses `1.0 / (remainder + epsilon)`, which is a powerful, non-linear convex function that aggressively rewards exact fits but suffers from instability and poor gradient behavior for large gaps.

My proposed algorithm combines the **Best Fit** logic with a **scale-invariant** penalty. Instead of using raw `remainder` or raw `1/remainder`, I calculate a `waste_ratio = remainder / item`. This ratio represents how much *extra* space is left relative to the item size. A ratio of 0 is a perfect fit; a ratio of 1 means half the bin is wasted; a ratio of 2 means the bin has 3x the item size, etc.

The priority score is designed as:
`score = -waste_ratio^2 - waste_ratio`

Why this works:
1.  **Convexity**: Squaring the ratio heavily penalizes large gaps. Placing a small item in a huge bin (high ratio) is strongly discouraged compared to linear penalties.
2.  **Scale Invariance**: It treats a 1% waste the same whether the item is size 1 or 1000, allowing the heuristic to generalize across different input distributions.
3.  **Gradient**: The derivative is `-2*ratio - 1`, which is always negative, ensuring that smaller remainders always get higher priority, but with a sharp "knee" near perfect fits.
4.  **Tie-breaking**: For items of similar size, we prefer filling larger bins slightly more aggressively? No, actually, in Best Fit, we just want the tightest fit. However, to break ties between two bins with identical relative waste (e.g., item=1, bin=2 vs item=1, bin=2 is impossible in a set, but item=2, bin=3 (ratio 0.5) vs item=2, bin=5 (ratio 1.5)), the relative metric handles it. If relative waste is identical, we prefer the *larger* absolute bin to preserve smaller bins for smaller future items? Or do we prefer the smaller absolute bin to close it? Usually, preserving smaller bins is better because large items can't go into small bins. So, if ratios are equal, we prefer the bin with larger absolute capacity? Wait, if item=1, bin=2 (rem=1) and item=1, bin=3 (rem=2). Ratio 1 and 2. Ratio 1 is better. So we pick bin 2. This is standard Best Fit.

Let's refine the formula to ensure stability and finite outputs.
`ratio = (bins - item) / item`
`score = -ratio^2 - 0.5 * ratio`

The `0.5 * ratio` linear term provides a consistent gradient for small ratios, while the quadratic term dominates for large ratios.

Idea: The algorithm calculates a scale-invariant priority score based on the relative waste of placing the item into each bin. It computes the `waste_ratio` as `(bin_capacity - item) / item`. The priority score is then defined as a convex penalty function of this ratio: `score = -(waste_ratio ** 2) - 0.5 * waste_ratio`. This formulation strongly penalizes bins that leave a large gap relative to the item size, effectively implementing a robust Best Fit heuristic that adapts to the magnitude of the item. By normalizing the remainder by the item size, the heuristic avoids the scaling issues of absolute remainders and the numerical instability of inverse functions used in the reference. The quadratic term ensures that even slight increases in relative waste significantly reduce the priority, driving the selection toward the tightest possible fit to minimize fragmentation and maximize bin density.

### 校准 Idea

The implemented algorithm computes a priority score for each feasible bin by applying a convex penalty to the scale-invariant waste ratio resulting from placing the item in that bin. The core decision rule is derived from the calculation of the waste ratio, defined as the difference between the bin's remaining capacity and the item size, normalized by the item size ($waste\_ratio = (bins - item) / item$). This normalization renders the heuristic scale-invariant, ensuring that the relative inefficiency of a placement is evaluated consistently regardless of the absolute magnitude of the item or the bins. This addresses the limitation of absolute best-fit strategies, which may fail to generalize when item sizes vary drastically, by transforming the problem into a comparison of relative inefficiency rather than absolute capacity.

The priority score is then computed as $score = -(waste\_ratio)^2 - 0.5 \times waste\_ratio$. This function is monotonically decreasing with respect to the waste ratio, ensuring that bins with smaller relative gaps always receive higher priority. The quadratic term $-(waste\_ratio)^2$ introduces a convex penalty that aggressively discourages placements that leave significant proportional space unused. For instance, while a linear penalty would penalize a 2x waste ratio twice as much as a 1x ratio, the quadratic term penalizes it four times as much, effectively driving the selection toward the tightest possible fit to minimize fragmentation. The linear term $-0.5 \times waste\_ratio$ provides a consistent gradient for small ratios, ensuring stable discrimination between bins with similar tight fits. By combining these terms, the algorithm creates a sharp "knee" in the priority landscape near perfect fits (where the waste ratio approaches zero), strongly favoring exact or near-exact matches while rapidly diminishing the priority of bins that leave larger proportional gaps.

Two specific conditions modify the standard computation to ensure robustness. First, if the input array of bins is empty, an empty float array is returned. Second, if the item size is negligible (less than $10^{-9}$), the algorithm defaults to a standard best-fit strategy using the negative of the bin capacity ($-bins\_f$). This avoids division by zero or numerical instability when normalizing by a near-zero item size, and correctly prioritizes the smallest available bins for infinitesimal items. Finally, any non-finite values in the resulting score array are replaced with a large negative constant ($-10^{10}$) to guarantee finite outputs as required by the contract. Relative to the parent code, the primary implementation change is the removal of the weighted linear primary score ($-remainder$) and the reduction of the quadratic term's influence. The final code exclusively relies on the scale-invariant quadratic and linear penalties of the waste ratio, abandoning the hybrid approach that combined absolute and relative metrics. This simplification aims to leverage the superior generalization properties of the relative metric over the absolute one, hypothesizing that the pure convex penalty on the waste ratio will better minimize the total number of bins used by preventing the premature exhaustion of large bins on small items.

### 最终代码

```python
import numpy as np

def priority(item: float, bins: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    """Returns priority with which we want to add item to each bin.
    Args:
        item: Size of item to be added to the bin.
        bins: Array of capacities for each bin.
    Return:
        Array of same size as bins with priority score of each bin.
    """
    # Convert to float to avoid integer division issues and ensure finite output
    bins_f = np.asarray(bins, dtype=np.float64)
    item = float(item)

    # Handle empty input
    if bins_f.size == 0:
        return np.array([], dtype=np.float64)

    # Handle negligible item size to avoid division by zero
    # If item is very small, we just want to fit it in the smallest available bin (Best Fit)
    # to avoid wasting large bins on tiny items.
    if item < 1e-9:
        # Return negative capacity so smallest capacity gets highest priority
        return -bins_f

    # Calculate the remainder after placing the item
    remainder = bins_f - item

    # Calculate the ratio of wasted space to the item size.
    # Since bins are feasible, remainder >= 0.
    # We use max(item, 1e-9) in denominator just in case, though item > 1e-9 is checked above.
    waste_ratio = remainder / item

    # Priority score:
    # We want to minimize waste_ratio.
    # Using a convex penalty: -ratio^2 - 0.5 * ratio
    # This ensures that tighter fits (lower ratio) have higher (less negative) scores.
    # The quadratic term heavily penalizes large gaps.

    score = -(waste_ratio ** 2) - 0.5 * waste_ratio

    # Ensure all values are finite (though they should be given the constraints)
    score = np.where(np.isfinite(score), score, -1e10)

    return score
```

```python
import numpy as np

def priority(item: float, bins: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    """Returns priority with which we want to add item to each bin.
    Args:
        item: Size of item to be added to the bin.
        bins: Array of capacities for each bin.
    Return:
        Array of same size as bins with priority score of each bin.
    """
    bins_f = np.asarray(bins, dtype=np.float64)
    item = float(item)

    if bins_f.size == 0:
        return np.array([], dtype=np.float64)

    # Handle edge case for very small items to avoid division by zero or instability
    if item <= 1e-9:
        # For negligible items, just use Best Fit (smallest remainder)
        # Score is higher for smaller bins
        return -bins_f

    # Calculate remainder (wasted space after placing item)
    remainder = bins_f - item

    # 1. Primary: Absolute Best Fit
    # Strongly prefer bins with least remaining capacity
    # Weight 1.0 is standard, but we might scale if needed.
    # Using -remainder directly.
    primary_score = -remainder

    # 2. Secondary: Relative Waste Penalty
    # Penalize bins where the gap is large relative to the item size.
    # This prevents "wasting" a large portion of a large bin on a small item.
    # ratio = remainder / item

    eps = 1e-6
    ratio = remainder / (item + eps)
    # Limit ratio to avoid extreme values if item is tiny, though item > 1e-9 is handled
    # A coefficient of 0.1 is chosen to be a secondary influence to primary_score
    secondary_score = -0.1 * (ratio ** 2)

    # 3. Tertiary: Global Consolidation / Fill Ratio
    # Prefer bins that are already "fuller" relative to the max bin.
    # This helps saturate bins to effectively reduce the count of active bins.
    max_cap = np.max(bins_f)
    if max_cap <= 1e-9:
        fill_ratio = np.zeros_like(bins_f)
    else:
        # fill_ratio is 1.0 for the max bin, 0.0 for the smallest bin
        # We want to prefer bins with HIGHER fill (closer to max cap? No, closer to full).
        # Actually, if we normalize by max_cap, a bin with capacity C has fill C/max_cap.
        # We want to prefer bins that are already large? Or bins that are nearly full?
        # In bin packing, "filling up" a bin means it has less remaining capacity.
        # The term (1 - bins_f / max_cap) is small for large bins and large for small bins.
        # This term prefers SMALLER bins. This aligns with Best Fit.
        # Does it add value?
        # Let's try a different angle: Prefer bins that are "almost full" in absolute terms?
        # Actually, simple Best Fit already does this.
        # Let's stick to a term that rewards bins with lower remaining capacity relative to the bin's original size?
        # We don't have original size.

        # Let's use a term that encourages using bins with moderate remaining capacity?
        # No, let's stick to the previous idea but refine the weight.
        # The previous code had 0.05 * (1 - bins_f / max_cap).
        # Let's increase the influence of the relative waste penalty instead, as that's a known improvement over pure Best Fit.

        pass

    # Let's refine the strategy.
    # Pure Best Fit (-remainder) is strong.
    # Adding -0.5 * (remainder/item)^2 helps.

    # Let's try:
    # Score = -remainder - lambda * (remainder / item)^2

    # If we just use the two terms above:
    total_score = primary_score + secondary_score

    # To avoid numerical issues and ensure finite values
    total_score = np.where(np.isfinite(total_score), total_score, -1e10)

    return total_score
```

## 09 online_bin_packing_r2_e232

算子 Fuse；节点 229；父节点 203；fitness -728.0；局部纠错：未计入。

增益：补充 running max、混合阈值与自适应 penalty 的计算。

保留问题或边界：两版把 bins.size 当成已打开箱数。评估器传入所有当前可容纳 item 的箱，包括未使用的空箱；该数量不能解释为已开箱数量或当前装箱密度。二次版也未给出父代关键系数差异。

<details><summary>原始 Idea、校准 Idea、最终代码与父代码</summary>

### 前置 Idea

Build a stateful, adaptive Best-Fit score with a dead-zone penalty and a "large-gap" preservation bonus, combining the reference's hybrid threshold and adaptive penalty strength with the current version's centered tie-break and finite-clamping. Track the running average and the maximum item size to compute a hybrid dead-zone threshold `T = max(avg, 0.5*max_seen)`. The base priority is `-residuals` (tighter fits win). For residuals inside the dead-zone `(0, T)`, subtract an adaptive penalty that is scaled by how many bins are open (consolidate more aggressively when many bins exist). For residuals `>= T`, add a small bonus proportional to the residual (and to the number of open bins) so that, when a tight-but-wasteful fit and a loose-but-useful fit are both available, the algorithm prefers keeping genuinely usable free space rather than fragmenting it. Keep a tiny centered index tie-break for determinism and clamp non-finite values.

### 校准 Idea

The implemented algorithm computes a composite priority score for each feasible bin to guide item placement in online one-dimensional bin packing, aiming to minimize the total number of bins. The decision rule is based on maximizing a score that primarily favors tight fits but adapts its behavior based on the historical distribution of item sizes and the current number of open bins. The core calculation begins with a base Best-Fit term, where the priority is initialized as the negative residual capacity ($ - (b_i - \text{item}) $), ensuring that bins with the smallest remaining space after placing the item receive the highest base priority. This aligns with the standard Best-Fit heuristic, which minimizes wasted space immediately.

To refine this base preference, the algorithm introduces two adaptive components derived from running statistics. It maintains global counters for the sum, count, and maximum of all previously processed items. These statistics inform a hybrid dead-zone threshold $ T $, defined as $\max(\text{avg\_item}, 0.5 \times \text{max\_seen})$, where $\text{avg\_item}$ is the running average (floored at 1.0) and $\text{max\_seen}$ is the largest item size observed so far. This threshold represents the minimum gap size considered "useful" for future items. The algorithm then applies a dead-zone penalty to any bin where the residual capacity falls within the interval $(0, T)$. Specifically, if a residual is small but nonzero, a penalty proportional to $(T - \text{residual})$ is subtracted from the priority. This penalty discourages creating tiny, fragmented gaps that likely cannot accommodate future typical items, thereby encouraging consolidation of items into fewer bins. The strength of this penalty is adaptive, increasing slightly as the number of open bins grows, which promotes more aggressive consolidation when the packing is less dense.

Conversely, for bins where the residual capacity is at least $ T $, the algorithm applies a large-gap bonus. This bonus adds a small positive value proportional to the residual size to the priority score. This mechanism slightly favors keeping larger, usable gaps open rather than filling them with items that leave only small, wasteful fragments. This is a strategic trade-off: while Best-Fit usually minimizes immediate waste, this bonus acknowledges that preserving large gaps can be beneficial for accommodating larger items later, potentially reducing the need to open new bins. Finally, a tiny, centered index-based tie-breaker is added to ensure deterministic selection among bins with identical computed priorities, and the resulting array is clamped to ensure all values are finite floats. The net effect is a dynamic Best-Fit variant that penalizes inefficient small gaps and rewards preserving large, useful capacity, adapting its aggressiveness based on the observed item size distribution and bin count.

### 最终代码

```python
import numpy as np

_sum_item = 0.0
_count_item = 0
_max_item_seen = 0.0


def priority(item: float, bins: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    """Returns priority with which we want to add item to each bin.

    Args:
        item: Size of item to be added to the bin.
        bins: Array of capacities for each bin (only feasible bins are passed).

    Return:
        Array of same size as bins with priority score of each bin.
    """
    global _sum_item, _count_item, _max_item_seen

    # Update workload statistics (stateful adaptation over the sequence).
    _sum_item += item
    _count_item += 1
    if item > _max_item_seen:
        _max_item_seen = item

    avg_item = _sum_item / _count_item if _count_item > 0 else 1.0
    if avg_item < 1.0:
        avg_item = 1.0

    # Cast to float before any float arithmetic / bonuses / penalties.
    bins_f = bins.astype(np.float64)
    residuals = bins_f - item

    # Base Best-Fit term: prefer the tightest fit (smallest residual).
    priorities = -residuals

    # Hybrid dead-zone threshold: useful gaps should be at least on the order of
    # a typical item, and also react to the largest item seen so far.
    threshold = max(avg_item, 0.5 * _max_item_seen)

    n = bins_f.size

    # Adaptive penalty factor: penalize tiny-but-nonzero gaps more when many
    # bins are open (pushes consolidation into fewer bins).
    factor = 1.0 + 0.5 * min(n / 10.0, 1.0)

    # Dead-zone penalty: discourage leaving gaps that are too small to be
    # useful for future (typically ~avg-sized) items.
    eps = 1e-9
    dead_mask = (residuals > eps) & (residuals < threshold)
    penalty_terms = np.where(dead_mask, factor * (threshold - residuals), 0.0)
    priorities = priorities - penalty_terms

    # Large-gap bonus: when a fit leaves a genuinely useful gap (>= threshold),
    # slightly prefer preserving that usable space rather than fragmenting it.
    large_mask = residuals >= threshold
    large_bonus = 0.1 * (1.0 + min(n / 10.0, 1.0)) * residuals
    priorities = priorities + np.where(large_mask, large_bonus, 0.0)

    # Tiny centered tie-break for determinism (does not change distinct rankings).
    if n > 1:
        idx = np.arange(n, dtype=np.float64)
        tie_break = (idx - (n - 1) / 2.0) * 1e-12
        priorities = priorities + tie_break

    # Ensure finiteness.
    priorities = np.where(np.isfinite(priorities), priorities, -1e9)

    return priorities
```

### 父代码

```python
import numpy as np

_sum_item = 0.0
_count_item = 0

def priority(item: float, bins: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    """Returns priority with which we want to add item to each bin.
    Args:
        item: Size of item to be added to the bin.
        bins: Array of capacities for each bin.
    Return:
        Array of same size as bins with priority score of each bin.
    """
    global _sum_item, _count_item

    # Update running average of item sizes (adaptive threshold)
    _sum_item += item
    _count_item += 1
    avg_item = _sum_item / _count_item if _count_item > 0 else 1.0
    if avg_item < 1.0:
        avg_item = 1.0

    # Cast to float to avoid integer issues and ensure finite float output
    b = bins.astype(np.float64)

    # Residual (slack) after placing the item; feasible bins have b >= item
    residuals = b - item

    # Base Best Fit: prefer smallest residual (largest negative residual is worst,
    # so we use -residuals so tighter fit = higher priority)
    priorities = -residuals

    # Adaptive dead-zone penalty: residuals in (eps, threshold) are "wasteful"
    # small gaps that likely can't fit a typical future item. Penalize them
    # linearly so the algorithm prefers exact fits (residual ~ 0) or gaps
    # large enough to be useful (residual >= threshold).
    threshold = avg_item
    eps = 1e-9
    dead_mask = (residuals > eps) & (residuals < threshold)
    factor = 2.0
    penalty_terms = np.where(dead_mask, factor * (threshold - residuals), 0.0)
    priorities = priorities - penalty_terms

    # Minimal deterministic tie-breaker: tiny index-based epsilon that never
    # overrides a real priority difference but ensures stable argmax on ties.
    n = b.size
    if n > 1:
        idx = np.arange(n, dtype=np.float64)
        tie_break = (idx - (n - 1) / 2.0) * 1e-12
        priorities = priorities + tie_break

    # Guarantee finiteness
    priorities = np.where(np.isfinite(priorities), priorities, -1e9)

    return priorities
```

</details>

## 10 online_bin_packing_r3_e68

算子 Refine；节点 67；父节点 38；fitness -744.5；局部纠错：未计入。

增益：展开 0.9 与 0.1 两项公式和类型转换。

保留问题或边界：二次版强化错误：声称改变严格 Best Fit 决策。实际 s=0.1+0.8/(1+gap)，仍严格偏好最小 gap。

<details><summary>原始 Idea、校准 Idea、最终代码与父代码</summary>

### 前置 Idea

The primary weakness of the pure Best-Fit heuristic (current algorithm) is its tendency to "greedily" pack items into the tightest possible slots, which often leads to fragmented bins with small, unusable remainders that cannot accommodate future items, especially if the item sequence has a specific structure (like decreasing sizes or a mix of large and small items). To improve fitness, I propose a "Robust Best-Fit" strategy that still prioritizes tight fits but adds a small incentive for bins with slightly more remaining space to mitigate fragmentation.

Specifically, the priority score is calculated as a weighted combination of two signals:
1.  **Best-Fit Signal**: `1 / (1 + leftover)`. This strongly rewards bins with minimal remaining space, ensuring high utilization. It is bounded and smooth.
2.  **Stability Signal**: `leftover / (1 + leftover)`. This provides a small secondary reward for bins that leave more space, acting as a soft tie-breaker that prefers larger remainders when the difference in leftover space is small. This helps prevent the creation of many tiny, useless gaps.

The final score is `0.9 * Best_Fit_Signal + 0.1 * Stability_Signal`. This weighting ensures that the "tightness" of the fit is the dominant factor (preserving the core strength of Best-Fit for minimizing bin count), while the "stability" term subtly steers the algorithm away from creating extremely small leftovers when a slightly larger (but still small) leftover is available, potentially reducing the number of bins needed later. This approach is more robust than the linear `-leftover` score because it handles ties and near-ties more intelligently and avoids the extreme fragmentation behavior of pure Best-Fit in certain sequences.

### 校准 Idea

The implemented algorithm defines a composite priority function that balances tightness of fit against residual stability for online one-dimensional bin packing. For each feasible bin, the function calculates the leftover capacity after placing the item, ensuring non-negative values through clamping. The priority score is a convex combination of two bounded signals: a "best-fit" term defined as $1 / (1 + \text{leftover})$ and a "stability" term defined as $\text{leftover} / (1 + \text{leftover})$. These are weighted by constants $\alpha = 0.9$ and $\beta = 0.1$, respectively. The best-fit term monotonically decreases with leftover space, assigning the highest priority to bins that minimize remaining capacity, which aligns with the standard Best-Fit heuristic for maximizing utilization. The stability term monotonically increases with leftover space, acting as a soft counter-bias that favors bins with slightly larger remainders. Because $\alpha > \beta$, the tightness of the fit remains the dominant factor in the decision process. However, the inclusion of the stability term modifies the strict monotonicity of pure Best-Fit; while the overall function generally favors smaller leftovers, the marginal utility of reducing leftover space is slightly tempered by the gain in stability for larger remainders. This creates a scenario where bins with extremely small leftovers (e.g., 0 or 1) are not overwhelmingly prioritized over bins with slightly larger leftovers (e.g., 2 or 3), provided the difference in best-fit scores is small. This adjustment aims to prevent the fragmentation of bins into numerous tiny, unusable gaps, which can occur in pure Best-Fit implementations when dealing with specific item size distributions. By casting inputs to float64, the implementation ensures numerical stability and compliance with the requirement for finite floating-point outputs, avoiding integer division issues or overflow risks that might arise in the parent code’s linear approach.

Relative to the parent code, the primary implementation change is the replacement of a linear scoring function with a non-linear, weighted composite score. The parent code assigned priority as $-\text{leftover}$, which strictly enforces Best-Fit selection, always choosing the bin with the absolute minimum remaining space. This linear approach offers no flexibility to account for the long-term utility of the remaining space; it treats a leftover of 1 identically in relative preference to a leftover of 100000000 (scaled by the same factor), purely based on magnitude. The final code introduces non-linearity via rational functions, bounding the scores between 0 and 1. This boundedness prevents extreme priority values that could dominate the selection if bin capacities vary widely in magnitude. More importantly, the addition of the stability term ($\beta \cdot \text{leftover} / (1+\text{leftover})$) introduces a preference for larger residuals, which is the opposite of the parent code's intent. While the parent code's linear penalty penalizes larger leftovers linearly, the final code's composite score penalizes larger leftovers sub-linearly for the best-fit component but rewards them sub-linearly for the stability component. This structural change shifts the decision rule from a strict "smallest leftover wins" to a "balanced tightness and stability wins." Additionally, the final code explicitly clamps the leftover to zero, safeguarding against numerical precision errors or unexpected negative values from the input array, whereas the parent code assumed feasibility and did not include this defensive measure. These changes are intended to improve robustness against fragmentation, potentially reducing the total number of bins used in sequences where strict Best-Fit leads to inefficient packing.

### 最终代码

```python
import numpy as np

def priority(item: float, bins: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    """Returns priority with which we want to add item to each bin.
    Args:
        item: Size of item to be added to the bin.
        bins: Array of capacities for each bin.
    Return:
        Array of same size as bins with priority score of each bin.
    """
    # Cast to float to ensure finite floating-point output and avoid integer issues
    bins_float = np.asarray(bins, dtype=np.float64)

    # Calculate remaining capacity after placing the item
    # Since bins are feasible, leftover should be >= 0, but we clamp to be safe
    leftover = np.maximum(bins_float - float(item), 0.0)

    # Calculate the two components of the priority score
    # 1. Best-Fit component: High score for small leftovers (tight fit)
    #    Uses a smooth decay to avoid division by zero and keep values bounded.
    best_fit_score = 1.0 / (1.0 + leftover)

    # 2. Stability/Fragmentation-Avoidance component:
    #    Slightly prefers larger leftovers to avoid creating tiny, unusable gaps.
    #    This is bounded in [0, 1) and grows with leftover.
    stability_score = leftover / (1.0 + leftover)

    # Combine the scores.
    # We heavily favor Best-Fit (90%) but use Stability (10%) to break
    # ties and prevent excessive fragmentation of bins into small residuals.
    alpha = 0.9
    beta = 0.1

    priority_scores = alpha * best_fit_score + beta * stability_score

    return priority_scores
```

### 父代码

```python
import numpy as np

def priority(item: float, bins: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    """Returns priority with which we want to add item to each bin.
    Args:
        item: Size of item to be added to the bin.
        bins: Array of capacities for each bin.
    Return:
        Array of same size as bins with priority score of each bin.
    """
    bins_float = np.asarray(bins, dtype=np.float64)
    # Best Fit: prefer the bin that leaves the least remaining space.
    # Since bins[i] >= item (feasible), leftover = bins[i] - item >= 0.
    # We want to maximize priority, so we assign higher priority to smaller leftover.
    # priority = -leftover = -(bins[i] - item) = item - bins[i]
    # Since item is constant for all bins, this is equivalent to -bins[i].
    # Using -leftover directly makes the intent clearer.
    leftover = bins_float - float(item)
    return -leftover
```

算子 Fuse；节点 241；父节点 226；fitness -730.75；局部纠错：未计入。

保留问题或边界：这属于新增有价值的事实，原版只描述算法，没有明确声称已改变父代，所以单列为识别无功能变化，而不算纠错。

<details><summary>原始 Idea、校准 Idea、最终代码与父代码</summary>

### 前置 Idea

The algorithm adopts an adaptive Best-Fit strategy that dynamically adjusts its preference for tight fits versus fragmentation avoidance based on the current density of the bin system. By calculating the median remaining waste among feasible bins, it determines if the system is "spacious" or "tight." In spacious conditions, it heavily penalizes fits that leave small, unusable gaps (waste < item size) to preserve large contiguous spaces for future larger items, while in tight conditions, it favors density with a mild penalty. A significant bonus is applied to perfect fits to ensure they are always selected, and a small index-based tie-breaker ensures determinism.

### 校准 Idea

The implemented algorithm employs an adaptive Best-Fit heuristic to minimize bin usage in online one-dimensional bin packing. The core decision rule prioritizes bins with the smallest remaining waste after placing the item, calculated as the difference between the bin's capacity and the item size. This base preference is derived directly from the term `-waste`, which assigns higher scores to tighter fits, theoretically reducing overall space consumption. However, the algorithm dynamically modifies this preference based on the system's current density to prevent fragmentation. It calculates the median waste across all feasible bins to determine the operational regime. If the median waste exceeds the item size, the system is classified as "spacious," indicating an abundance of free space; in this mode, bins where the remaining waste is less than the item size (creating unusable gaps) are heavily penalized by subtracting $2.5 \times \text{item\_size}$ from their score. This aggressive penalty encourages the algorithm to skip "nearly full" bins in favor of those with larger contiguous free spaces, preserving flexibility for future larger items. Conversely, if the median waste is below the item size, the system is "tight," and only a mild penalty of $0.25 \times \text{item\_size}$ is applied to fragmented bins, shifting the focus toward maximizing density. Additionally, a fixed bonus of 10.0 is added to bins that provide a perfect fit (zero waste), ensuring they are almost always selected over other options. To guarantee deterministic behavior in the event of exact score ties, a negligible index-based penalty ($10^{-6} \times \text{index}$) is applied, favoring earlier bins in the array. Infeasible bins are assigned a constant negative value ($-10^9$) to ensure they are never selected. The algorithm returns a floating-point array of the same shape as the input, with integer inputs cast to float to support the arithmetic operations and bonuses.

Relative to the parent code, the final code maintains the same logical structure and algorithmic core but introduces extensive explanatory comments and minor stylistic adjustments for clarity. Specifically, the variable names and logic flow remain identical: the calculation of `bins_float`, `item_float`, `feasible` masks, `waste`, `median_waste`, and the adaptive penalty mechanism are unchanged. The primary differences are in the documentation; each major step in the final code is annotated with detailed comments explaining the rationale behind the calculations, such as why the median waste determines the penalty magnitude and how the tie-breaker ensures determinism. For instance, the final code explicitly states that the "Spacious regime" aims to "aggressively avoid fragmentation" and the "Tight regime" aims to "favor density," mirroring the design idea more explicitly in the code comments. The actual numerical parameters (penalty factors 2.5 and 0.25, bonus 10.0, tie-breaker coefficient $10^{-6}$, and negative infinity value $-10^9$) and the conditional logic for determining the regime based on `median_waste > item_float` are preserved exactly as in the parent code. Therefore, the functional behavior is identical, and the changes are purely cosmetic and documentary, enhancing readability without altering the algorithm's performance characteristics.

### 最终代码

```python
import numpy as np

def priority(item: float, bins: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    """Returns priority with which we want to add item to each bin.
    Args:
        item: Size of item to be added to the bin.
        bins: Array of capacities for each bin.
    Return:
        Array of same size as bins with priority score of each bin.
    """
    # Ensure inputs are float for calculation
    bins_float = np.asarray(bins, dtype=np.float64)
    item_float = float(item)

    # Initialize with a very low priority for infeasible bins
    NEG_INF = -1e9
    scores = np.full(bins_float.shape, NEG_INF, dtype=np.float64)

    # Identify feasible bins (those that can hold the item)
    feasible = bins_float >= item_float

    if not np.any(feasible):
        return scores

    feasible_indices = np.where(feasible)[0]
    feasible_bins = bins_float[feasible_indices]

    # Calculate waste (remaining space after placing item)
    waste = feasible_bins - item_float

    # Base score: Best Fit (prefer smallest waste)
    current_scores = -waste

    # Calculate median waste to determine regime
    median_waste = np.median(waste)

    # Identify fragmented bins: waste is > 0 but < item size
    # These create gaps that can't fit another item of this size
    is_fragmented = (waste > 0) & (waste < item_float)

    if np.any(is_fragmented):
        # Adaptive penalty based on overall system density
        if median_waste > item_float:
            # Spacious regime: aggressively avoid fragmentation to preserve large gaps
            penalty = 2.5 * item_float
        else:
            # Tight regime: mild penalty, favor density
            penalty = 0.25 * item_float

        current_scores[is_fragmented] -= penalty

    # Perfect fit bonus: strongly prefer zero waste
    perfect = (waste == 0)
    if np.any(perfect):
        current_scores[perfect] += 10.0

    # Add a tiny index-based tie-breaker to ensure determinism
    # Favor earlier indices in case of exact ties
    idx_order = np.arange(len(feasible_indices), dtype=np.float64)
    tie_breaker = -1e-6 * idx_order

    final_scores = current_scores + tie_breaker
    scores[feasible_indices] = final_scores

    return scores
```
